## GHSA-667r-xxjv-c9mm · fastify

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 8.1 · Pattern: `TYPE_CONFUSION→BYPASS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an incorrect handling of asynchronous validator results. When an async validator returned a value, the framework treated it as a synchronous custom compiler result, attempting to unwrap properties like 'error' or 'value'. This allowed an attacker to craft a payload with these keys to inject a validation error or replace the entire request part.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass validation rules or inject arbitrary data into the request body, potentially leading to other vulnerabilities or unexpected application behavior.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/validation.js
+++ b/lib/validation.js
@@ -305,8 +305,13 @@ function validateParam (validatorFunction, request, paramName) {
   }
 
   if (ret && typeof ret.then === 'function') {
+    // An async validator (for example an `$async` Ajv schema) resolves with the
+    // validated data itself, not a `{ value, error }` result. Its properties must
+    // not be unwrapped the way a synchronous custom compiler result is, otherwise
+    // a payload carrying an `error` or `value` key could inject a validation error
+    // or replace the whole request part. Treat the async result as pass/fail only.
     return ret
-      .then((res) => { return answer(res) })
+      .then((res) => { return res === false ? validatorFunction.errors : false })
       .catch(err => { return err }) // return as simple error (not throw)
   }
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the handling of asynchronous validator results. Instead of attempting to unwrap properties, it now treats the async result as a simple pass/fail. If the result is 'false', it returns the validator's errors; otherwise, it returns 'false' (indicating no error). This prevents an attacker from injecting validation errors or replacing the request part.

## GHSA-3q6v-r5mr-hxv8 · league/commonmark

2026-09-30 · PHP · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an inefficient string search operation within the GitHub Flavored Markdown Table extension. The `strpos($paragraph, '|')` check was performed on the entire growing paragraph content in a loop, leading to a quadratic time complexity as the input size increased.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious Markdown input that, when parsed, would consume excessive CPU resources, potentially leading to a denial of service for applications using the affected library.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/Extension/Table/TableStartParser.php
+++ b/src/Extension/Table/TableStartParser.php
@@ -33,7 +33,15 @@ public function __construct(int $maxAutocompletedCells = TableParser::DEFAULT_MA
     public function tryStart(Cursor $cursor, MarkdownParserStateInterface $parserState): ?BlockStart
     {
         $paragraph = $parserState->getParagraphContent();
-        if ($paragraph === null || \strpos($paragraph, '|') === false) {
+        if ($paragraph === null) {
+            return BlockStart::none();
+        }
+
+        // Only the paragraph's last line can be the header row, so limit the quick check to it.
+        // Scanning the whole (growing) paragraph on every line would be quadratic.
+        $lastLineBreak = \strrpos($paragraph, "\n");
+        $lastLine      = $lastLineBreak === false ? $paragraph : \substr($paragraph, $lastLineBreak + 1);
+        if (\strpos($lastLine, '|') === false) {
             return BlockStart::none();
         }
```

### Fix summary

The patch optimizes the string search by first extracting only the last line of the paragraph before checking for the presence of a pipe character (`|`). This change ensures that the `strpos` operation is performed on a much smaller, fixed-size string, preventing the quadratic time complexity.

## GHSA-cxp5-3px4-pw24 · com.fasterxml.jackson.core:jackson-databind

2026-09-30 · Java · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The `CollectionDeserializer` and `MapDeserializer` in Jackson-databind used an inefficient algorithm (O(N^2)) for resolving forward references during deserialization. Specifically, when resolving N references, the existing implementation would iterate through a list of pending references for each resolution, leading to quadratic time complexity if references resolved in reverse order.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious JSON payload with a large number of forward references that resolve in a specific order, causing the deserialization process to consume excessive CPU resources and time, leading to a denial of service for applications processing such input.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/main/java/com/fasterxml/jackson/databind/deser/std/CollectionDeserializer.java
+++ b/src/main/java/com/fasterxml/jackson/databind/deser/std/CollectionDeserializer.java
-        private List<CollectionReferring> _accumulator = new ArrayList<CollectionReferring>();
+        private final List<Object> _pending = new ArrayList<>();
+        private int _pendingStart;
+        private final Map<Object, Deque<CollectionReferring>> _unresolvedById = new HashMap<>();
```

### Fix summary

The patch refactors the `CollectionReferringAccumulator` and `MapReferringAccumulator` to use a `Deque` (double-ended queue) for efficient lookup of unresolved references by ID. It also introduces a `_pending` list and `_pendingStart` index to efficiently move resolved values to the result collection, avoiding repeated merging and ensuring O(N) performance for resolution.

## GHSA-g5vv-9gxw-82hx · GitPython

2026-09-30 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the use of a complex regular expression (name_email_regex) with nested quantifiers and overlapping groups to parse author/committer fields. When presented with specially crafted input strings, this regex would exhibit catastrophic backtracking, leading to an exponential increase in processing time.

### Impact

An attacker could supply a malicious string to the affected parsing function, causing the application to consume excessive CPU resources and time, effectively leading to a denial of service for the process handling the input.

### Key diff

```
-	name_email_regex = re.compile(r"(.*) <(.*?)>")
-	m = cls.name_email_regex.search(string)
-	if m:
+	left_bracket = line.find("<")
+	right_bracket = line.find(">", left_bracket + 1)
+	if left_bracket >= 0 and right_bracket >= 0:
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the problematic regular expressions and replaces the parsing logic with a simpler, iterative string manipulation approach using `find()` and `partition()`. This change avoids the use of regular expressions for this specific parsing task, thereby eliminating the ReDoS vulnerability.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 24 occurrences**
> Languages: Go, JavaScript, Python, PHP, Java

## GHSA-9c5c-9qcx-q35q · @nestjs/platform-fastify

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the `@nestjs/platform-fastify` adapter's `middie` engine not properly decoding URL paths before matching them against defined middleware regular expressions. An attacker could use URL-encoded characters in the path to bypass path-scoped middleware, as the matching logic would operate on the encoded path, which might not match the expected pattern.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass security middleware, authentication checks, or other path-scoped restrictions, potentially gaining unauthorized access to protected resources or functionalities that were intended to be guarded by the middleware.

### Key diff

```
--- packages/platform-fastify/adapters/middie/fastify-middie.ts
+++ /dev/null
-          const decodedUrl = safeDecodeURI(
-            sanitizedUrl,
-            (that.initialConfig?.routerOptions as any)?.useSemicolonDelimiter ||
-              that.initialConfig?.useSemicolonDelimiter,
-          ).path;
-          const result = regexp.exec(decodedUrl);
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the custom `middie` engine implementation from `@nestjs/platform-fastify`. Instead, it now relies on the official `@fastify/middie` plugin, which includes the necessary fix to decode URL paths before matching them against middleware regular expressions, preventing bypasses via encoded characters.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 24 occurrences**

## GHSA-m9gg-hp2v-232j · @grpc/grpc-js

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The `getAuthContext` method and the `Http2Transport` class in `@grpc/grpc-js` did not properly check the authorization status of the TLS socket. They would proceed to extract and return peer certificate information if a `TLSSocket` was present, even if the socket itself was not authorized, leading to an incorrect representation of the connection's security context.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially present an unauthorized certificate, and the application would incorrectly perceive it as authorized, leading to a bypass of authentication or authorization checks that rely on the `getAuthContext` output or the `authContext` in `Http2Transport`.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/grpc-js/src/server-interceptors.ts
+++ b/packages/grpc-js/src/server-interceptors.ts
@@ -1020,15 +1020,17 @@ export class BaseServerInterceptingCall
     return this.host;
   }
   getAuthContext(): AuthContext {
-    if (this.stream.session?.socket instanceof TLSSocket) {
-      const peerCertificate = this.stream.session.socket.getPeerCertificate();
-      return {
-        transportSecurityType: 'ssl',
-        sslPeerCertificate: peerCertificate.raw ? peerCertificate : undefined
-      }
-    } else {
+    if (!(this.stream.session?.socket instanceof TLSSocket)) {
+      return {};
+    }
+    if (!this.stream.session.socket.authorized) {
       return {};
     }
+    const peerCertificate = this.stream.session.socket.getPeerCertificate();
+    return {
+      transportSecurityType: 'ssl',
+      sslPeerCertificate: peerCertificate.raw ? peerCertificate : undefined
+    }
   }
   getConnectionInfo(): ConnectionInfo {
     return this.connectionInfo;
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds an explicit check for `session.socket.authorized` before retrieving and returning peer certificate information in both `getAuthContext` and `Http2Transport`. This ensures that certificate details are only exposed if the TLS connection has been successfully authorized.

## GHSA-42vr-xj54-vc7v · PyJWT

2026-09-30 · Python · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The application did not properly handle a RecursionError that could occur during the JSON parsing of the JWT payload. An attacker could craft a malicious JWT payload that, when parsed by `json.loads`, would trigger a RecursionError, leading to an unhandled exception and application crash.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could send a specially crafted JWT to the application, causing it to crash and resulting in a denial of service for legitimate users. This could disrupt the availability of services relying on PyJWT.

### Key diff

```
--- a/jwt/api_jwt.py
+++ b/jwt/api_jwt.py
@@ -296,7 +296,7 @@ def _decode_payload(self, decoded: dict[str, Any]) -> dict[str, Any]:
         """
         try:
             payload: dict[str, Any] = json.loads(decoded["payload"])
-        except ValueError as e:
+        except (ValueError, RecursionError) as e:
             raise DecodeError(f"Invalid payload string: {e}") from e
         if not isinstance(payload, dict):
             raise DecodeError("Invalid payload string: must be a json object")
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `_decode_payload` method to explicitly catch `RecursionError` in addition to `ValueError` during the JSON parsing of the JWT payload. When a `RecursionError` occurs, it is now caught and re-raised as a `DecodeError`, preventing an unhandled exception and allowing the application to gracefully handle the malformed input.

## GHSA-3pq3-5fj3-cg6v · axios

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The Axios HTTP/2 adapter, specifically when using the `fetch` adapter, did not properly respect configured DNS lookup and proxy settings. This allowed an attacker to bypass intended network controls by making requests directly, rather than routing them through the specified proxy or resolving DNS via the configured mechanism.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass network restrictions, potentially accessing internal resources or services that should not be reachable from the internet, leading to information disclosure or further compromise of the internal network.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/adapters/http.js
+++ b/lib/adapters/http.js
@@ -252,7 +252,7 @@ function setProxy(options, configProxy, location, isRedirect, configHttpsAgent, configHttpAgent) {
   let proxy = configProxy;
   const proxyEnvAgent = getProxyEnvAgent(options, configHttpAgent, configHttpsAgent);
-  if (!proxy && proxy !== false && !isNodeEnvProxyEnabled(proxyEnvAgent)) {
+  if (!proxy && proxy !== false && allowEnvProxy && !isNodeEnvProxyEnabled(proxyEnvAgent)) {
     const proxyUrl = getProxyForUrl(location);
     if (proxyUrl) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `fetch` adapter to ensure that `fetchOptions` are correctly processed and that `maxRedirects` is honored. It also updates the `http` adapter to explicitly pass an `allowEnvProxy` flag to the `setProxy` function, preventing environment proxies from being used when not intended, thus enforcing configured proxy settings.

## GHSA-542g-h47m-68v8 · axios

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an unhandled 'error' event in the HTTP/2 client's `ClientHttp2Session` initialization. When an HTTP/2 session encountered an error during setup, the error event was not properly caught, leading to the Node.js process crashing and resulting in a Denial of Service.

### Impact

An attacker could trigger an unhandled error in the HTTP/2 client, causing the Node.js application using Axios to crash. This would lead to a Denial of Service, making the application unavailable to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/adapters/http.js
+++ b/lib/adapters/http.js
@@ -123,16 +123,14 @@ function isNodeEnvProxyEnabled(agent, nodeVersion = process.versions && process.
   const agentOptions = agent && agent.options;
 
   return Boolean(
-    agentOptions &&
-      utils.hasOwnProp(agentOptions, 'proxyEnv') &&
-      agentOptions.proxyEnv != null
+    agentOptions && utils.hasOwnProp(agentOptions, 'proxyEnv') && agentOptions.proxyEnv != null
   );
 }
 
 function getProxyEnvAgent(options, configHttpAgent, configHttpsAgent) {
   return isHttps.test(options.protocol)
-    ? (configHttpsAgent || https.globalAgent)
-    : (configHttpAgent || http.globalAgent);
+    ? configHttpsAgent || https.globalAgent
+    : configHttpAgent || http.globalAgent;
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the issue by ensuring that the 'error' event on `ClientHttp2Session` is always handled. It adds an explicit error listener to prevent unhandled exceptions from crashing the process, thus mitigating the Denial of Service vulnerability.

## GHSA-8988-9cw3-xx77 · urllib3

2026-09-30 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `INSECURE_DEFAULT→CONFIG`

### Root cause

The urllib3 library incorrectly prioritized the connection pool's SSL context and related TLS configuration parameters over the specific SSL context provided for an HTTPS proxy. This meant that even if a user configured a strict TLS policy for the proxy connection, it could be overridden or ignored by a less strict policy from the general connection pool.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially bypass intended TLS security configurations for HTTPS proxy connections, leading to man-in-the-middle attacks where the client might connect to a malicious proxy without proper certificate validation, or use weaker TLS versions/ciphers than intended.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/urllib3/connection.py
+++ b/src/urllib3/connection.py
@@ -838,30 +838,30 @@ def connect(self) -> None:
             # fall back to using the connection's SSL context until
             # urllib3 v3.0. Appropriate warning is emitted in
             # ``ProxyManager.__init__``.
-            if self.proxy_is_forwarding and self.proxy_config is not None:
-                ssl_context = self.proxy_config.ssl_context
-            else:
-                ssl_context = self.ssl_context
-
-            sock_and_verified = _ssl_wrap_socket_and_match_hostname(
-                sock=sock,
-                cert_reqs=self.cert_reqs,
-                ssl_version=self.ssl_version,
-                ssl_minimum_version=self.ssl_minimum_version,
-                ssl_maximum_version=self.ssl_maximum_version,
-                ca_certs=self.ca_certs,
-                ca_cert_dir=self.ca_cert_dir,
-                ca_cert_data=self.ca_cert_data,
-                cert_file=self.cert_file,
-                key_file=self.key_file,
-                key_password=self.key_password,
-                server_hostname=server_hostname_rm_dot,
-                ssl_context=ssl_context,
-                tls_in_tls=tls_in_tls,
-                assert_hostname=self.assert_hostname,
-                assert_fingerprint=self.assert_fingerprint,
-            )
-            self.sock = sock_and_verified.socket
+            wrapped_socket: ssl.SSLSocket | SSLTransport
+            if self.proxy_is_forwarding and self.proxy_config is not None:
+                wrapped_socket = self._connect_tls_proxy(self.host, sock)
+                is_verified = self.proxy_is_verified is True
+            else:
+                wrapped_socket, is_verified = _ssl_wrap_socket_and_match_hostname(
+                    sock=sock,
+                    cert_reqs=self.cert_reqs,
+                    ssl_version=self.ssl_version,
+                    ssl_minimum_version=self.ssl_minimum_version,
+                    ssl_maximum_version=self.ssl_maximum_version,
+                    ca_certs=self.ca_certs,
+                    ca_cert_dir=self.ca_cert_dir,
+                    ca_cert_data=self.ca_cert_data,
+                    cert_file=self.cert_file,
+                    key_file=self.key_file,
+                    key_password=self.key_password,
+                    server_hostname=server_hostname_rm_dot,
+                    ssl_context=self.ssl_context,
+                    tls_in_tls=tls_in_tls,
+                    assert_hostname=self.assert_hostname,
+                    assert_fingerprint=self.assert_fingerprint,
+                )
+            self.sock = wrapped_socket
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `_connect_tls_proxy` method to explicitly use the proxy's SSL context and associated TLS parameters (like `cert_reqs`, `ca_certs`, `ssl_version`) when establishing a TLS connection to an HTTPS proxy. If no proxy-specific SSL context is provided, it falls back to the general connection pool's settings, but the explicit proxy configuration is now prioritized.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 38 occurrences**
> Languages: JavaScript, Go, C#, PHP, Erlang, Python, Java, RUST, Ruby

## GHSA-c29m-xwm3-cm6r · axios

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed due to an inefficient regular expression used in the `fromDataURI` data: URL parser within Axios. When processing a specially crafted, excessively long data URI, the regex could enter a catastrophic backtracking state, consuming significant CPU resources.

### Impact

An attacker could send a request with a malicious data URI, causing the Node.js event loop to freeze and leading to a Denial of Service (DoS) for the application using Axios.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff does not contain the relevant code changes for the ReDoS vulnerability. It focuses on other parts of the Axios library, specifically the `fetch` and `http` adapters, which are unrelated to the `fromDataURI` parser.
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not directly show the fix for the ReDoS vulnerability in `fromDataURI`. The changes shown are related to `fetch.js` and `http.js` adapters, primarily adjusting how `fetch` options are handled, setting default request options, and refining proxy logic. The actual ReDoS fix would involve optimizing the problematic regular expression.

## GHSA-c2m8-h5v5-343r · tornado

2026-09-30 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The `StaticFileHandler` in Tornado did not properly resolve symlinks when serving static files. While it checked if the requested path was within the root directory, this check was performed on the *unresolved* path. An attacker could create a symlink within the static root pointing to an arbitrary location outside of it, allowing them to bypass the initial path validation.

### Impact

An attacker could read arbitrary files on the server's filesystem that the Tornado process has read access to, by crafting a request that traverses out of the intended static file directory via a symlink.

### Key diff

```
--- a/tornado/web.py
+++ b/tornado/web.py
@@ -2995,6 +3016,15 @@ def validate_absolute_path(self, root: str, absolute_path: str) -> Optional[str]
         # the requested path so a request to root/ will match.
         if not (absolute_path + os.path.sep).startswith(root):
             raise HTTPError(403, "%s is not in root static directory", self.path)
+        # The check above is on the path as written, which is cheap and
+        # rejects traversal without touching the filesystem. Resolve
+        # symlinks only once it has passed, so that the remaining checks -
+        # and the path this method returns - refer to the file that will
+        # actually be opened rather than to the link. The two checks
+        # cannot be merged: this one must stay on the unresolved path,
+        # because allowed_symlink_directory may be wider than the root and
+        # would then let a ../ traversal through.
+        absolute_path = self._resolve_symlink_target(absolute_path)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `_resolve_symlink_target` method that explicitly resolves symlinks and verifies that the *resolved* path remains within an `allowed_symlink_directory` (which defaults to the static root). This check is performed after the initial path validation but before serving the file, preventing symlink-based path traversal.

## GHSA-ff3f-86qr-9cv3 · @angular/router

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from how V8's JavaScript engine optimizes object properties. When an object frequently receives numeric keys, V8 might switch its internal representation from a dictionary to an array-like structure for performance. If an attacker provided a URL with a large number of distinct, large numeric matrix parameters, this could force V8 to allocate an extremely large backing store (array) for the `params` object, even if most indices were empty, leading to excessive memory consumption.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious URL that, when processed by an Angular SSR application, would consume an inordinate amount of memory, leading to a denial of service for the server. This could crash the application or exhaust server resources, making it unavailable to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/router/src/url_tree.ts
+++ b/packages/router/src/url_tree.ts
@@ -723,7 +740,7 @@ class UrlParser {
       }
     }
 
-    params[decode(key)] = decode(value);
+    setUrlDerivedKey(params, decode(key), decode(value));
   }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new helper function, `setUrlDerivedKey`, which strategically sets and then deletes a sentinel property (`SLOW_ELEMENTS_SENTINEL`) on objects before assigning numeric keys. This action signals to V8 that the object should maintain a dictionary-based internal representation for its properties, preventing it from allocating oversized array-like backing stores for sparse numeric keys. This ensures that large numeric URL parameters do not trigger excessive memory allocation.

## GHSA-j8rh-479h-cp32 · axios

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→HEADER`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from Axios's `fetch` adapter not properly sanitizing or isolating user-provided `fetchOptions` when constructing the `Request` object. Specifically, certain sensitive options like `body`, `headers`, `method`, `signal`, `duplex`, and `credentials` could be inherited or overridden from `fetchOptions`, leading to unintended behavior or injection if an attacker could control these values.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially inject arbitrary headers into the outgoing request, manipulate the request method, or alter other critical fetch options, which could lead to various issues such as bypassing security controls, data leakage, or unexpected server-side behavior.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/adapters/fetch.js
+++ b/lib/adapters/fetch.js
@@ -420,20 +436,57 @@ const factory = (env) => {
       // Set User-Agent header if not already set (fetch defaults to 'node' in Node.js)
       headers.set('User-Agent', 'axios/' + VERSION, false);
 
-      const resolvedOptions = {
-        ...fetchOptions,
+      const safeFetchOptions =
+        fetchOptions == null ? fetchOptions : Object.assign(Object.create(null), fetchOptions);
+
+      if (safeFetchOptions) {
+        // These options are owned by Axios and are already reflected in the
+        // resolved Request passed to fetch.
+        delete safeFetchOptions.body;
+        delete safeFetchOptions.headers;
+        delete safeFetchOptions.method;
+        delete safeFetchOptions.signal;
+        delete safeFetchOptions.duplex;
+        delete safeFetchOptions.credentials;
+      }
+
+      const resolvedOptions = Object.assign(Object.create(null), safeFetchOptions, {
         signal: composedSignal,
         method: method.toUpperCase(),
         headers: toByteStringHeaderObject(headers.normalize()),
         body: data,
         duplex: 'half',
         credentials: isCredentialsSupported ? withCredentials : undefined,
-      };
+      });
+
+      if (isRequestSupported) {
+        utils.forEach(DEFAULT_REQUEST_OPTIONS, (value, key) => {
+          if (resolvedOptions[key] === undefined) {
+            resolvedOptions[key] = value;
+          }
+        });
+
+        if (resolvedOptions.signal === undefined) {
+          resolvedOptions.signal = null;
+        }
+
+        if (resolvedOptions.body === undefined) {
+          resolvedOptions.body = null;
+        }
+      }
+
+      if (maxRedirects === 0) {
+        resolvedOptions.redirect = 'manual';
+
+        if (safeFetchOptions) {
+          safeFetchOptions.redirect = 'manual';
+        }
+      }
 
       request = isRequestSupported && new Request(url, resolvedOptions);
 
       let response = await (isRequestSupported
-        ? _fetch(request, fetchOptions)
+        ? _fetch(request, safeFetchOptions)
         : _fetch(url, resolvedOptions));
 
       const responseHeaders = AxiosHeaders.from(response.headers);
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `DEFAULT_REQUEST_OPTIONS` constant to ensure a baseline for fetch options. It explicitly filters out sensitive properties from user-provided `fetchOptions` before merging them into the final `resolvedOptions` for the `Request` object. This prevents user-controlled `fetchOptions` from directly overriding critical internal request parameters.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 22 occurrences**

## GHSA-m8m8-qj5v-23w3 · axios

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PROTOTYPE_POLLUTION→OVERRIDE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from Axios's Node.js HTTP adapter allowing an attacker to inject properties into `Object.prototype` via inherited `createConnection` options. This was possible because the adapter did not properly sanitize or isolate user-controlled options when creating HTTP/HTTPS agents, leading to a prototype pollution gadget.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve request socket hijacking, potentially redirecting network requests to arbitrary destinations or intercepting sensitive data by controlling the `createConnection` method or other critical properties inherited from `Object.prototype`.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/adapters/fetch.js
+++ b/lib/adapters/fetch.js
@@ -420,7 +436,7 @@ const factory = (env) => {
       headers.set('User-Agent', 'axios/' + VERSION, false);
 
       const resolvedOptions = {
-        ...fetchOptions,
+      const safeFetchOptions = fetchOptions == null ? fetchOptions : Object.assign(Object.create(null), fetchOptions);
+      const resolvedOptions = Object.assign(Object.create(null), safeFetchOptions, {
         signal: composedSignal,
         method: method.toUpperCase(),
         headers: toByteStringHeaderObject(headers.normalize()),
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the prototype pollution by ensuring that `fetchOptions` and `safeFetchOptions` are created as null-prototype objects (`Object.create(null)`). It also introduces a new `allowEnvProxy` parameter to the `setProxy` function in the HTTP adapter, providing more granular control over proxy configuration and preventing unintended inheritance of properties.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 33 occurrences**

## GHSA-mghh-pgcx-3jjj · axios

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an inefficient regular expression used in the `shouldBypassProxy` function for host normalization. When processing untrusted redirect `Location` headers, a specially crafted hostname could lead to a catastrophic backtracking scenario, causing the regex engine to consume excessive CPU time.

### Impact

An attacker could trigger a Denial of Service (DoS) by providing a malicious `Location` header in a redirect response, leading to high CPU utilization and unresponsiveness of the server processing the request.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/adapters/http.js
+++ b/lib/adapters/http.js
@@ -252,13 +251,22 @@ function isSameOriginRedirect(redirectOptions, requestDetails) {
  * @param {http.ClientRequestArgs} options
  * @param {AxiosProxyConfig} configProxy configuration from Axios options object
  * @param {string} location
+ * @param {boolean} [allowEnvProxy=true] whether environment proxy configuration can be used
  *
- * @returns {http.ClientRequestArgs}
+ * @returns {boolean} whether a proxy applies to the selected transport
  */
-function setProxy(options, configProxy, location, isRedirect, configHttpsAgent, configHttpAgent) {
+function setProxy(
+  options,
+  configProxy,
+  location,
+  isRedirect,
+  configHttpsAgent,
+  configHttpAgent,
+  allowEnvProxy = true
+) {
   let proxy = configProxy;
   const proxyEnvAgent = getProxyEnvAgent(options, configHttpAgent, configHttpsAgent);
-  if (!proxy && proxy !== false && !isNodeEnvProxyEnabled(proxyEnvAgent)) {
+  if (!proxy && proxy !== false && allowEnvProxy && !isNodeEnvProxyEnabled(proxyEnvAgent)) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the ReDoS vulnerability by introducing a new `allowEnvProxy` parameter to the `setProxy` function. This parameter, when set to `false`, prevents the use of environment proxy configuration, thereby avoiding the vulnerable `shouldBypassProxy` function when processing untrusted redirect URLs. Additionally, the `fetch` adapter now explicitly sets `redirect: 'manual'` when `maxRedirects` is 0, preventing automatic redirects and thus bypassing the vulnerable proxy logic for such cases.

## GHSA-r4gj-5m52-g5wh · axios

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The Axios `fetch` adapter did not properly enforce the `maxRedirects: 0` configuration option. When `maxRedirects` was set to 0, the underlying `fetch` API was still allowed to follow redirects by default, leading to an unintended redirection.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass the `maxRedirects: 0` setting to force Axios to follow redirects, potentially leading to Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) by redirecting requests to internal network resources or sensitive endpoints.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/adapters/fetch.js
+++ b/lib/adapters/fetch.js
@@ -436,6 +450,13 @@ const factory = (env) => {
         }
       }
 
+      if (maxRedirects === 0) {
+        resolvedOptions.redirect = 'manual';
+
+        if (safeFetchOptions) {
+          safeFetchOptions.redirect = 'manual';
+        }
+      }
 
       request = isRequestSupported && new Request(url, resolvedOptions);
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `DEFAULT_REQUEST_OPTIONS` object to ensure consistent default fetch options. Crucially, it explicitly sets the `redirect` option to `'manual'` in `resolvedOptions` when `maxRedirects` is 0, preventing the `fetch` API from automatically following redirects.

## GHSA-vcvr-r3jv-pc5j · next

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the `next/og` ImageResponse component in Next.js did not properly sanitize user-supplied input before processing it. This allowed for the injection of malicious SVG content, which could then execute arbitrary code.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve remote code execution on the server hosting the Next.js application, potentially leading to full system compromise, data exfiltration, or denial of service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/next/src/compiled/@vercel/og/index.edge.js
+++ b/packages/next/src/compiled/@vercel/og/index.edge.js
@@ -2271,52 +2318,8 @@ var require_hex_rgb = __commonJS({
   }
 });
 
-// node_modules/.pnpm/escape-html@1.0.3/node_modules/escape-html/index.js
-var require_escape_html = __commonJS({
-  "node_modules/.pnpm/escape-html@1.0.3/node_modules/escape-html/index.js"(exports2, module) {
-    "use strict";
-    var matchHtmlRegExp = /["'&<>]/;
-    module.exports = escapeHtml;
-    function escapeHtml(string) {
-      var str = "" + string;
-      var match = matchHtmlRegExp.exec(str);
-      if (!match) {
-        return str;
-      }
-      var escape;
-      var html = "";
-      var index = 0;
-      var lastIndex = 0;
-      for (index = match.index; index < str.length; index++) {
-        switch (str.charCodeAt(index)) {
-          case 34:
-            escape = "&quot;";
-            break;
-          case 38:
-            escape = "&amp;";
-            break;
-          case 39:
-            escape = "&#39;";
-            break;
-          case 60:
-            escape = "&lt;";
-            break;
-          case 62:
-            escape = "&gt;";
-            break;
-          default:
-            continue;
-        }
-        if (lastIndex !== index) {
-          html += str.substring(lastIndex, index);
-        }
-        lastIndex = index + 1;
-        html += escape;
-      }
-      return lastIndex !== index ? html + str.substring(lastIndex, index) : html;
-    }
-  }
-});
+// node_modules/.pnpm/satori@0.25.0/node_modules/satori/dist/standalone.js
+var import_escape_html = __toESM(require_escape_html(), 1);
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the vulnerability by re-introducing and correctly applying the `escape-html` utility. This ensures that all user-provided input rendered within the `ImageResponse` component is properly HTML-escaped, preventing the injection of malicious SVG or other HTML content.

## GHSA-vxq7-64xx-v4gw · urllib3

2026-09-30 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The urllib3 library, when processing chunked-transfer-encoded HTTP responses, did not limit the length of the chunk-size line or the trailing CRLF line. A malicious server could send an extremely long or unterminated chunk-size line, causing the client to buffer an unbounded amount of data into memory.

### Impact

An attacker could exhaust the memory of a client application using urllib3, leading to a denial of service for that application.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/urllib3/response.py
+++ b/src/urllib3/response.py
@@ -1357,7 +1364,12 @@ def _update_chunk_length(self) -> None:
         # we'll try to read it from socket.
         if self.chunk_left is not None:
             return None
-        line = self._fp.fp.readline()  # type: ignore[union-attr]
+        line = self._fp.fp.readline(_MAX_CHUNK_LINE_LENGTH + 1)  # type: ignore[union-attr]
+        if len(line) > _MAX_CHUNK_LINE_LENGTH:
+            self.close()
+            raise ProtocolError(
+                "Response chunk size line exceeded maximum allowed length"
+            ) from None
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a maximum length for chunk-size lines and chunk trailer lines (_MAX_CHUNK_LINE_LENGTH). It modifies the readline calls to respect this limit and raises a ProtocolError if the line length exceeds the maximum, preventing unbounded memory consumption.

## GHSA-x97p-jq2g-jp4f · axios

2026-09-30 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PROTOTYPE_POLLUTION→OVERRIDE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the `axios.toFormData` function's options handling. It was possible to inject arbitrary properties into the `options` object, which could then be used to pollute the prototype of `Object.prototype` or other critical objects, leading to unexpected behavior or further attacks.

### Impact

An attacker could manipulate object prototypes, potentially leading to denial of service, remote code execution in specific environments, or other application logic bypasses depending on how the polluted properties are later used in the application.

### Key diff

```
-      const resolvedOptions = {
-        ...fetchOptions,
+      const safeFetchOptions =
+        fetchOptions == null ? fetchOptions : Object.assign(Object.create(null), fetchOptions);
+
+      const resolvedOptions = Object.assign(Object.create(null), safeFetchOptions, {
         signal: composedSignal,
         method: method.toUpperCase(),
         headers: toByteStringHeaderObject(headers.normalize()),
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces `Object.assign(Object.create(null), ...)` when handling `fetchOptions` and `resolvedOptions`. This ensures that options objects are created without a prototype or with a clean prototype chain, preventing attacker-controlled properties from polluting `Object.prototype` or other global objects.
